\hypertarget{abstract}{%
\chapter*{ABSTRACT}\label{abstract}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{ABSTRACT}

Narratives on politically contentious topics, such as racism and gun violence, trigger polarizing attitudes that impede equitable policy solutions while exacerbating patterns of systemic harm against historically-marginalized communities. This dissertation adopts community gun violence as a problem space for conducting a mixed-methods investigation of how to leverage the power of critical consciousness (i.e., understanding how intersecting systems of power influence behavior and societal outcomes) to increase public support for responses seeking to repair inequities and empower affected youth to engage with protective, prosocial behaviors. In Chapter 1, a series of online experimental studies examine how critical narratives on gun violence in some "poor communities of color" can shift racial attitudes and policy preferences across political orientation. Chapter 2 presents the development and proposed experimental evaluation of a critical narrative on youth violence prevention using design-thinking and participatory methods. Compared to traditional youth behavior change narratives, the critical narrative seeks to promote protective behaviors by reframing gun carrying as playing into racist narratives and scaffolding civic engagement as an empowering path to fight back.

\hypertarget{policy-factor-analyses}{%
\section{Policy Factor Analyses}\label{policy-factor-analyses}}

\hypertarget{all-policies}{%
\subsection{All Policies}\label{all-policies}}

\textbf{Punitive}:
* COPS: More police on the streets
* JAILTIME: Longer jail terms for those convicted of violent crimes
* PENALTY: Increase criminal penalties for non-violent crimes
* PRISONS: More prisons and fewer opportunities for parole

\textbf{Restorative}:
* BANBOX: Remove criminal-history questions from employment applications
* YOUTH: More community programs for young people
* JOBS: Job programs for people in the inner-cities
* FEDAID: More federal assistance to the poor

  JOBS: Job programs for people in the inner-cities
\item
  FEDAID: More federal assistance to the poor
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{punitive-support}{%
\section{Punitive Support}\label{punitive-support}}

Participants were asked rate the extent to which they oppose or favor four items on a scale from 1 (strongly oppose) to 4 (strongly favor):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  COPS: More police on the streets
\item
  JAILTIME: Longer jail terms for those convicted of violent crimes
\item
  PENALTY: Increase criminal penalties for non-violent crimes
\item
  PRISONS: More prisons and fewer opportunities for parole
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{restorative-support}{%
\section{Restorative Support}\label{restorative-support}}

Participants were asked rate the extent to which they oppose or favor four items on a scale from 1 (strongly oppose) to 4 (strongly favor):

\emph{Restorative items}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  More job and community programs for young people
\item
  Job programs for people in the inner-cities
\item
  More federal assistance to the poor
\item
  Remove criminal-history questions from employment applications
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{punitive-support-1}{%
\section{Punitive Support}\label{punitive-support-1}}

Participants were asked rate the extent to which they oppose or favor four items on a scale from 1 (strongly oppose) to 7 (strongly favor):

\emph{Punitive items}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  More police on the streets
\item
  Longer jail terms for those convicted of violent crimes
\item
  More prisons and fewer opportunities for parole
\item
  Increase criminal penalties for non-violent crimes (i.e., property
  crimes and theft)
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{restorative-support-1}{%
\section{Restorative Support}\label{restorative-support-1}}

Participants were asked rate the extent to which they oppose or favor four items on a scale from 1 (strongly oppose) to 7 (strongly favor):

\emph{Restorative items}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  More job and community programs for young people
\item
  Job programs for people in the inner-cities
\item
  More federal assistance to the poor
\item
  Remove criminal-history questions from employment applications
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{exploratory}{%
\section{Exploratory}\label{exploratory}}

\hypertarget{attitudes-toward-police-experts}{%
\subsection{Attitudes Toward Police Experts}\label{attitudes-toward-police-experts}}

\begin{quote}
The article you read included two perspectives on how to address gun violence in poor neighborhoods:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Police officials recommended increased law enforcement, stronger penalties, more aggressive prosecutions, police technology, and partnering with neighborhoods for crime tips.
\item
  Policy researchers recommended increasing investments in housing, local business, education, and police-community relations.
\end{itemize}

In your opinion, when it comes to deciding how to address gun violence, how TRUSTWORTHY are police officials?

In your opinion, when it comes to deciding how to address gun violence, how QUALIFIED are police officials?
\end{quote}

\hypertarget{attitudes-toward-policy-experts}{%
\subsection{Attitudes Toward Policy Experts}\label{attitudes-toward-policy-experts}}

\begin{quote}
The article you read included two perspectives on how to address gun violence in poor neighborhoods:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Police officials recommended increased law enforcement, stronger penalties, more aggressive prosecutions, police technology, and partnering with neighborhoods for crime tips.
\item
  Policy researchers recommended increasing investments in housing, local business, education, and police-community relations.
\end{itemize}

In your opinion, when it comes to deciding how to address gun violence, how TRUSTWORTHY are policy researchers?

In your opinion, when it comes to deciding how to address gun violence, how QUALIFIED are policy researchers?
\end{quote}

\hypertarget{argument-quality}{%
\subsection{Argument Quality}\label{argument-quality}}

\begin{quote}
The article you read includes the perspective that\ldots{}

``in order to reduce the gun violence problem in poor, (urban/inner-city) neighborhoods of color it is necessary to repair the economic devastation of those communities that was caused by decades of (bad/racist) policies.''

According to that perspective, investment in affordable housing, support for local businesses, and public education (among other things) are needed in order to improve gun violence in those neighborhoods.

In your opinion, how convincing was the argument for that perspective?

In your opinion, how well-supported was that perspective by facts mentioned in the article?
\end{quote}

\hypertarget{nfc}{%
\subsection{NFC}\label{nfc}}

\begin{quote}
\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Scale: Not at all (1) Slightly (2) Somewhat (3) A great deal (4) Almost completely (5)
\item
  I prefer complex to simple problems. (NFC\_SIMPLE)
\item
  I like to have the responsibility of handling a situation that requires a lot of thinking. (NFC\_RESP)
\item
  Thinking is not my idea of fun. (NFC\_NOTFUN)
\item
  I would rather do something that requires little thought than something that is sure to challenge my thinking abilities. (NFC\_NOCHALLENGE)
\item
  I try to anticipate and avoid situations where there is a likely chance that I will have to think in depth about something. (NFC\_AVOID)
\item
  I find satisfaction in deliberating hard for long hours. (NFC\_SATISFY)
\item
  I only think as hard as I have to. (NFC\_ONLY)
\item
  I prefer to think about small daily projects to long-term ones. (NFC\_DAILY)
\item
  I like tasks that require little thought once I've learned them. (NFC\_LITTLE)
\item
  The idea of relying on thought to make my way to the top appeals to me. (NFC\_RELY)
\item
  I really enjoy a task that involves coming up with new solutions to problems. (NFC\_SOLUTIONS)
\item
  Learning new ways to think doesn't excite me. (NFC\_UNEXCITE)
\item
  I prefer my life to be filled with puzzles that I must solve. (NFC\_PUZZLES)
\item
  The notion of thinking abstractly is appealing to me. (NFC\_ABSTRACT)
\item
  I would prefer a task that is intellectual, difficult, and important to one that is somewhat important but does not require much thought. (NFC\_DIFFICULT)
\item
  I feel relief rather than satisfaction after completing a task that required a lot of mental effort. (NFC\_RELIEF)
\item
  It's enough for me that something gets the job done; I don't care how or why it works. (NFC\_DONE)
\item
  I usually end up deliberating about issues even when they do not affect me personally. (NFC\_DELIBERATE)
\end{itemize}
\end{quote}

\hypertarget{study-3}{%
\chapter{STUDY 3}\label{study-3}}

\hypertarget{abstract-1}{%
\section*{Abstract}\label{abstract-1}}

What is it about the critical narrative that is driving the conditional effect on policy support? Study 3 tests the existence of a causal pathway, in which the critical narrative impacts policy support by prompting a shift towards attributing the high rates of gun violence in some Black neighborhoods on structural factors, rather than dispositional ones. To test whether this attributional shift mediates the critical narrative's impact, we ran a 4 x 1 between-subjects study in which I explore whether a critical narrative on gun violence impacts policy preferences by changing how readers attribute the cause of high rates of gun homicides in poor communities of color, compared to an individualized narrative, a naive anti-racist narrative, and a control. I report the extent to which participants attribute this problem to the residents' dispositional factors (e.g., lack of work ethic, poor values) versus structural societal factors (e.g., less job opportunities, underfunded schools). I found a main effect of condition on policy support and a zero-sum budget allocation task in our hypothesized direction. Participants in the critical condition reported less support for punitive policies and allocated a lower percentage of a violence prevention budget to policing versus community development. Analyses also indicated that participants in the critical narrative condition, compared to the control conditions, were more likely to attribute the problem to structural factors and less likely to attribute it to dispositional factors. Critical condition participants also expressed greater a willingness to share their respective policy memos on social media and a greater interest in signing a community-led petition advocating for restorative approaches to gun violence.

\hypertarget{policy-factor-analyses-2}{%
\section{Policy Factor Analyses}\label{policy-factor-analyses-2}}

\hypertarget{all-policies-2}{%
\subsection{All Policies}\label{all-policies-2}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  More police on the street to arrest criminals
\item
  Longer prison sentences and fewer opportunities for parole
\item
  Increase the severity of criminal penalties and fines
\item
  Invest in technology to identify and apprehend criminals
\item
  Invest in police-led community programs to increase crime tips
\item
  Seize property from people involved in gun-related crimes
\item
  More after-school programs to keep at-risk youth out of trouble
\item
  More federal assistance to the poor
\item
  More economic development programs to create jobs
\item
  Invest in public community resources to revitalize poor neighborhoods
\item
  Invest in re-entry programs to support ex-prisoners transition to society
\item
  Invest in community-led peacekeeping organizations
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{summary-of-past-measures}{%
\section{Summary of past measures}\label{summary-of-past-measures}}

\textbf{Punitive}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  COPS: More police on the streets
\item
  JAILTIME: Longer jail terms for those convicted of violent crimes
\item
  PENALTY: Increase criminal penalties for non-violent crimes
\item
  PRISONS: More prisons and fewer opportunities for parole
\item
  POLICE: More police on the street to arrest criminals
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Restorative (4 items)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  JOBS: Job programs for people in the inner-cities
\item
  PUBLIC: Invest in public community resources to revitalize poor neighborhoods
\item
  REENTRY: Invest in re-entry programs to support ex-prisoners transition to society
\item
  FEDAID: More federal assistance to the poor
\item
  YOUTH: More community programs for young people
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{time-1-measures}{%
\section{Time 1 Measures}\label{time-1-measures}}

\textbf{Punitive (4 items)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  COPS: More police on the streets
\item
  JAILTIME: Longer jail terms for those convicted of violent crimes
\item
  PENALTY: Increase criminal penalties for non-violent crimes
\item
  PRISONS: More prisons and fewer opportunities for parole
\item
  POLICE: More police on the street to arrest criminals
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Restorative (4 items)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  JOBS: Job programs for people in the inner-cities (change to poor areas)
\item
  PUBLIC: Invest in public community resources to revitalize poor neighborhoods
\item
  REENTRY: Invest in re-entry programs to support ex-prisoners transition to society
\item
  FEDAID: More federal assistance to the poor (change to federal assistance)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Share (1 items)}\label{share-1-items}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Would you\ldots{}
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{modern-racism-10-items}{%
\subsection{Modern Racism (10 items)}\label{modern-racism-10-items}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  10 itmes
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{time-2}{%
\section{Time 2}\label{time-2}}

Changes to throw off suspicion:
- scale
- wording (strongly for/against); increase/decrease
- formatting

We're going to ask you about 2 issues selected by us, and then the issue that is important to them.

So now we're going to ask you to rate your opinion on randomly selected questions. (fixed )

Do you think government investment in each of the following should be increased, decreased, or kept the same? (1=Increased, decreased, kept the same, don't know).

\begin{itemize}
\item
  building more toll roads by private companies to ease congestion
\item
  investment in public transportation like buses, trains, and high-speed rails
\item
  repairing and maintaining public roadways, bridges, and tunnels
\item
  expand and improve access to high speed internet in rural areas
\item
  publicly funded road expansions
\item
  update water and plumbing infrastructure; lead pipe removal
\item
  access to low-cost childcare for working parents
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{a-issue-priority}{%
\subsection{A) Issue priority}\label{a-issue-priority}}

\uline{Question wording:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  ``How concerned, if at all, are you personally about each of the
  following economic issues in the country today?''
\end{itemize}

\uline{Favorite items:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Economy (repub)
\item
  Violent crime (repub)
\item
  Climate and environment (dem; from Pew political value items)

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    investing in public transportation to cut down traffic and reduce pollution
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Healthcare (democrat)
\item
  Education (bipartisan)
\item
  Infrastructure

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    high speed rail lines
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Gun policy (bipartisan)
\end{itemize}

\uline{Other items:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Immigration (repub; cut?)
\item
  Energy policy (bipartisan; cut? or replace with climate/env?)
\item
  Future of democracy in the country (dem-ish; cut)
\item
  Policies about how election and voting work (dem-ish; cut)
\item
  Foreign policy (repub; cut)
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{b-policy-support}{%
\subsection{B) Policy support}\label{b-policy-support}}

\uline{From past studies}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  REST\_FEDAID: More federal assistance to the poor\\
\item
  REST\_JOBS: Job programs for people in the inner-cities
\item
  REST\_YOUTH: More community programs for young people
\item
  REST\_BANBOX: Remove criminal-history questions from employment applications
\item
  REST\_SCHOOL: More after-school programs to keep at-risk youth out of trouble
\item
  REST\_JOBECON: More economic development programs to create jobs
\item
  REST\_PUBLIC: Invest in public community resources to revitalize poor neighborhoods
\item
  PUN\_COPS: More police on the streets
\item
  PUN\_PENALTY: Increase criminal penalties for non-violent crimes
\item
  PUN\_PRISONS: More prisons and fewer opportunities for parole
\item
  PUN\_JAILTIME: Longer jail terms for those convicted of violent crimes
\item
  PUN\_POLICE: More police on the street to arrest criminals
\item
  PUN\_SEIZE: Seize property from people involved in gun-related crimes
\item
  PUN\_TIPS: Invest in police-led community programs to increase crime tips
\item
  PUN\_TECH: Invest in technology to identify and apprehend criminals
\item
  GUN\_CONTROL: Tighter restrictions on gun ownership and gun purchases
\item
  GUN\_CARRY: Allow open carry of legally purchased firearms
\end{itemize}

\uline{Welfare attitudes (Cooley et al., 2022; a = .78)}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  ``Most people on welfare could get by without it if they really tried
  (reversescored)''
\item
  ``People who stay on welfare have no desire to work (reversescored)
\item
  ``Welfare recipients are lazy on average (reversescored)
\item
  ``Welfare recipients tend to be ambitious''
\item
  ``In general, welfare recipients are hardworking.''
\end{enumerate}

\uline{Welfare policy support (Cooley et al., 2022; a =.79)}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  ``In general, the wealthy should be taxed at higher rates to provide
  benefits to the poor'' (1 = strongly disagree; 6 = strongly agree)
\item
  ``Imagine you had a say in making up the federal budget for the next
  fiscal year. Would you want more or less money dedicated to welfare
  programs?'' (1 = much less money; 6 = much more money)
\item
  ``How do you feel about raising federal income taxes for people who
  make more than \$200,000 (USD) per year to benefit public services
  for low income individuals?'' (1 = I greatly oppose this policy; 6 =
  I greatly support this policy).
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{c-racial-attitudes-cut}{%
\subsection{C) Racial attitudes (cut?)}\label{c-racial-attitudes-cut}}

\textbf{``In general in our country these days, would you say that black people
are treated less fairly than white people, white people are treated less
fairly than black people, or both are treated about equally in each of
the following situations?''}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  In hiring, pay and promotions
\item
  When applying for a loan or mortgage
\item
  In dealing with the police
\item
  By the criminal justice system
\item
  When voting in elections
\item
  When seeking medical treatment
\item
  In stores or restaurants (cut)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{``How much of a problem do you think each of the following are for
Black people living in the country today?''}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Racism
\item
  efforts to limit voting
\item
  economic inequality
\item
  quality of K-12 schools
\item
  police brutality
\item
  affordability of healthcare
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{d-responsibility-for-reparations}{%
\subsection{D) Responsibility for reparations}\label{d-responsibility-for-reparations}}

\begin{quote}
Asking about reparations may be a good way to see if the critical
condition increases recognition of racial discrimination in
intergenerational wealth-building to today's issues.
\end{quote}

RACESURV17: How much, if at all, do you think the legacy of slavery
affects the position of Black people in American society today?

REPARATIONS: Do you think descendants of people enslaved in the U.S.
should be repaid in some way, such as given land or money?

REP\_INST: How much responsibility, if any, do the following have for
repaying descendants of people enslaved in the U.S.?

\begin{itemize}
\item
  The US federal government
\item
  Businesses and banks that profited from slavery
\item
  Colleges and universities that benefited from slavery
\item
  Descendants of families who engaged in the slave trade
\end{itemize}

REP\_FORM: How helpful do you think each of the following types of
repayment would be for descendants of people enslaved in the U.S.?
(1=not at all helpful; 7=extremely helpful)

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Cash payments
\item
  Financial assistance for buying or remodeling a home
\item
  Financial assistance for starting or improving a business
\item
  Educational scholarships
\end{itemize}

EQUALEFFECT: How effective would the following be for helping Black
people move toward equality in the U.S.?

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Establishing a national Black political party
\item
  All elected officials governing Black neighborhoods are Black
\item
  Black people owning all businesses in Black neighborhoods
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{cutting-floor-notes-for-writeup}{%
\chapter{Cutting floor notes for writeup}\label{cutting-floor-notes-for-writeup}}

We could ask participants to rate issues by personal priority, and then ask to rate whether spending should increase or decrease. We're going to randomly pick 2 issues to ask Qs about. First is healthcare (preexisting conditions, affordable insurance) or domestic issues (high speed internet, traffic, etc).
How do you feel about investing in the following policies related to TRANSPORTATION:

Should government spending on dealing with X be increased, decreased, or kept the same? (1=Increased, decreased, kept the same, don't know)
